ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e194	In the hiking hall earlier today, the chairman of the committee in an interview with TV 2 said something like this: we are talking about the backbone of the welfare community itself. I thought that was very well said. I'm looking at Anundsen, sitting in the hall here. I want to start by talking a little bit about the Norwegian welfare state, or the Norwegian welfare society, which can be defined in many ways as a solidarity split layer: you're going to do your duty and you're going to demand your right, we're creating values and we're distributing them fairly. We're going to have a model that ensures all well-being, care, health, education and work. People's rights will ensure us through sickness and unemployment and as pensioners. This is also important as a starting point for the organisation of the public sector, which is to put this into practice. We know that the challenges of achieving good and efficient public sector service production will increase in the years to come, including: Because we get more older, we get higher demands on quality and on availability. There are great expectations of service providers, both from the political system and from users. We see that the requirements for the first-line service are increasing considerably, we see that the pressure on the first-line service is increasing, and we see that the focus on the first-line service is increasing – where users meet the public service service provider. The size, through our decisions, and the Government, as the executive, is responsible for the organisation of the public sector for the benefit of the users. People who need help and service should meet with respect when they come to a public office. It is important that users do not meet the wall, not to say the door, when they go to a public office. It is also important that they do not have to go to more than one door, that they come through on the phone, that they receive answers and that they receive a prompt and sound procedure. This is really the whole essence of public service. The establishment of Nav was based on a welfare policy vision, with a first-line job and welfare management service as an important tool for achieving the objective of a comprehensive labour and social policy. There is an overall increase – both today and several times earlier – that has joined the Nav reform. Let me underline it quite strongly. The starting point was – and is – more in work, fewer in social security, simpler for users and more efficiency. It has not been the job of the Control and Constitutional Committee to look at or take a decision on the organisational model. It's the trade committee's job. Our responsibility is to see whether the decisions and assumptions of the Storting have been followed up and, of course, to consider the Riksrevision report on the revision of Nav for the financial year 2008. Perhaps the implementation of the Survey Decision on Nav could be called an imbalance between objectives, resources and capacity for implementation. The political system probably did not take account of the scale or complexity of the reform that was launched. In the name of the post-war — and it is known to be a good reason — questions can be asked whether this was well communicated, whether it was created unrealistic expectations from the political side. Former State Councillor Bjarne Håkon Hanssen said at the hearing on 15 January 2010, i.e. a few weeks ago: ‘I don’t think anyone really fully understand how demanding it was.’ Perhaps it was sub-communicated that there would be a difficult transition phase, also because proceedings and services were maintained in parallel with demanding reform work. That doesn't mean Nav isn't going to work well. I would also like to stress that. To quote once again Hanssen, "I'm absolutely sure the history of Nav is going to be a success story." Let me add that what we are dealing with today is the financial year 2008, and that many of the circumstances pointed out by the Riksrevision were already addressed by the Council of State of Day Terje Andersen in St.prp. No 51 in the spring of 2009 and in the Innst of the Trade Union Committee. S. No 190. The Supervisory and Constitutional Committee shall assume that these measures have been followed up and shall be continued. The main thing from the Riksrevision is that they are critical of Nav’s deprioritisation over several years to ensure satisfactory compliance with the internal control of the performance management. Weak internal control deliberately opens up abuse of welfare systems, and we have been given examples of it as well. It is dramatic that the Riksrevision cannot confirm the 2008 accounts of the establishment, because the accounts contain errors and shortcomings. So we're talking about $300 billion. It's not $300 million, it's $300 billion, so of course it's dramatic. The National Audit Office also says that Nav lacks control parameters for key parts of its business. Among other things, the directorate lacks control data in relation to the objective of correct performance. The Committee assumes that the establishment has sound operational management which contributes to a positive development within the time frame, balances, user service and internal control. What's probably made the strongest impression in the months I've been working with Nav, are the countless recordings and, in part, the dissatisfaction that has been channelled into me and others from different categories of users of Nav. And we also had someone at the hearing. This is the first line service — or the local Nav office. It's about how the little man and woman meet a public office when they get on different errands. Despite the Storting's assumption of the opposite, both competence and decision-making authority have apparently moved from the local office up to the so-called administrative units behind the scenes, which otherwise also joined the Storing when treating St.prp. No 51. The Nav offices appear today as a unit that lacks broad competence. Increased frustration on the part of employees about not being able to make the simplest decisions and not being able to deal with or answer questions, makes it appear that there is a shift of case handlers from the first line service to offices that are protected from user contact. It's also serious. I was a little in doubt, but think I'll quote from a text post in the VG yesterday from Finn, Sagstua: «Yes, now it is ten weeks since I sent the travel bill to Nav in the Ski. There is only one stamp missing for payment, I was informed, but they expected a few more weeks. Excellent with the new system!’ I don't know if this is Sagstua in North Odal, but in that case it's far from North Odal to Skien. Or she who went to the Nav office on Skedsmo to get cards for blue prescription. It was forwarded to Eidsvoll and took six weeks. Now there is a matter that is now cleared up, because it is no longer going to happen in the Nav offices, but still. A unanimous committee stresses that it has been a prerequisite for the Nav reform that local offices should give one door to all services, and that the procedure should be reduced. I would like to commend the Council of State Bjurstrom for addressing this problem by initiating a thorough analysis of the division of labour between the Nav offices and the administrative units. During the consultation, there were also views on Nav’s competence in communication, vocational guidance and contact with working life. This was the competence of the old Aetat, how to ensure return to work. There are reports from several teams that these are competences that Nav is deprioritising today, that the employment service simply does not work. This applies to all types of jobseekers and to contacts between jobseekers and the business community, including in the case of higher-skilled workers. Of course, this is not the least about people with reduced working capacity. In the first case on the agenda, Innst. 142 S, we can read that while the workplaces have recruited so-called IA sites in 693 companies, Nav was only able to place 96. So there seems to be a misunderstanding here – that this contact between the jobseeker and the employer is not possible, because there are actually almost 600 places left for the companies to report to Nav. Finally, one of the reasons for malpayments and late processing seems to be still a lack of effective ICT solutions. The Committee points out that the out-of-date system leads to inefficiency and control challenges. Now we're working on a new ICT strategy. It's important, even when we know that the Riksaudition says that today's system does not meet the requirements of the Financial Regulation on the security and traceability of transactions. In the end, I would like to underline what Councillor Bjurstrøm said in the hearing: «The work to make Nav the organisation we all want must be done on a broad front.» We support the Nav reform, and in many places Nav also works very well. Let's not forget that. But there is a lot left before users get one door and a proper and quick procedure. Employees must be involved in this process. They've been hitched in the critique of Nav. However, the 17 000 staff have done a great job of keeping the team going through a very demanding reform process. It also belongs to the picture of Nav. When the State of Employment and Welfare continues to reorganise, and in this case the Directorate itself is involved, it is obvious that the employees' organisations, including those in the Directorate, will be provided with the participation to which the works of agreement refer. I assume that the Council of State contributes to that happening. There is generally consensus in this setting. However, the Progress Party believes that the Council of State should assess whether the Nav Director and the other management have the necessary trust and competence. From our point of view, I would like to stress that the Stortinget does not interfere with who the Council of State and the Department employ at all times. It is the Council of State that is responsible to us, not representatives of the government.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e312	The Nav reform is the biggest administrative reform we have implemented in modern times. It is important both because Nav manages the country's most important resource, the labour force, and because it manages the quality of life and the ability to live for people who live difficult lives or are too old or sick to work and have no income. Yes, not only is income dependent on people, but also necessary social follow-up when life becomes difficult and flocks to, as it sometimes does. Therefore, it is clear that such a reform, when it does not work optimally, will have a terrible impact on those involved. That is why the criticism brought by the Riksrevision is very serious. I would also like to remind you that we, who have followed the reform of the Trade Union Committee, have addressed this matter in budgetary proceedings after the period of criticism of the Riksrevision, and also in the form of our own cases to the Stortinget, which deals with exactly the same issues. This is why both the Government and the Storing have acted in time to deal with the problems that have occurred. The expectations were great. Yes, some people think that expectations were too big, and it may be that they were – that they underestimated such a big and complicated reform. I would like to remind you – because I have been working on these matters for 13 years now in Stortingen – that the starting point was not that there were establishments with full control, where everyone who needed assistance received it quickly and was treated properly and with respect. Unfortunately, this was not the case in advance either, but the problem increased when the reform was launched. But the goal of the reform was that it would be easier, that more people would get help in the past, that they would get better help in the near future, and that it would be easier to control the payments – that people would get what they were supposed to and nothing else. Everyone was concerned that it should be safe. Mispayments and fraud with the social security system are, in my view, no worse than cheating on the tax, but in the political debate around these systems, it will be seen as something worse and as a part of tearing down the confidence of the entire security system that people in difficult situations depend on. Therefore, it is very important that the whole Nav system has very good internal control and that they have law-abiding decision-making processes, where the error payments are as small as possible. That is why I am very pleased that this has now been taken more seriously. In this context, the size of the population dealt with in the previous ageing period a matter of what is called social security and introduced new rules to ensure this area. In the case of waiting time, it has been too long. Everyone understands that when you don’t get money – and you don’t have a salary anywhere else – the crisis is total. When this comes at the same time as a financial crisis, the situation becomes very serious. When Stortinget treated St.prp. No 51 for 2008-2009, this was one of the things we focused most on. Then new resources were allocated to address this. It was also looked at what's called the interface. I am, like previous speakers, very pleased with the signals from the Council of State on a review of the interface. What the Storting said about the treatment last time was that Nav could of course deal with completely simple issues in the first line, and so get through with the things that were simple. More complicated matters could have been said to have another loop. At the time, the unemployment benefit was involved, but the Stortinget also gave clear signals that there might be a need for this in other matters, which was confirmed both by the Council of State and by the Director of Nav during consultations when we discussed the matter. So there's a need to look at it. There is a need to simplify where it can be simplified and there is a need for good quality control procedures in areas where required. What has also been addressed is the situation related to ICT and the additional problems it poses for establishments that need to merge and deal with many different ICT systems, which are sometimes not entirely feasible to work together. Clearly, this is a major challenge. There is not so much political discussion in this matter, but it is a little strange that those who are the sharpest critics both in terms of lack of funding for ICT and in terms of Nav not having mastered its tasks, it is the party that has managed to cut a billion in the Nav operating budget in the two worst years. It is a little difficult to take that criticism seriously when you see that both Nav was assigned to larger tasks, and you took from them the resources to do the job. I am quite convinced that with that kind of budgeting, the situation would have been considerably worse, both for the employees and, not least, for the users. I think it should motivate us all to a little bit of sense when, for ideological reasons, we go into public administration, and especially on the administration that exists for those who have the most difficulty, with a general savings knife – that it's kind of just cutting a little here and there, because in this organization there's so much dough meat. The National Audit Office's report and all the messages we get, both from users and from employees in this organization, tell us that here is an organisation that has had more than enough to do, and more tasks than it has for a period of time had resources. I agree with the representative Foss that the matter that was referred to on the radio today was not a pleasant matter. It can demonstrate an error priority, but it can also demonstrate that there have been processes that have gone too fast or have not been planned well enough, so that users get into trouble, as the Storting assumed they should not. I would like briefly to say something about the work centres, which is part of another report. The National Audit Office's report also reveals something that we are told about every day, namely that some of the work centres work very well. Where they function well, they contribute significantly to the faster return of people after illness, to their entry into the labour market, and to employers feeling that they are being given competent assistance in their work related to health and illness in the workplace. This is why it is very important to systematise the work of work centres in such a way that this good tool works well throughout the country. Finally, I would like to say that I am very pleased with the signals from the Council of State during the hearing that concerned the steps she is now taking to improve the situation in Nav in the future, and that she will look at the organisational conditions, as well as the need for a review of competence, which the Committee has unanimously underlined over and over again. It is necessary to have the necessary skills and staff in the first line. That's where the users meet the service, and that's also where the moment of truth's judgment on the Nav reform will come: Will it be good, or will it become bad? I am convinced that the Nav reform will be good, but I am also convinced that we need to spend more resources on this in the future, and that this needs stronger political governance than it has been now. I hear that the Council of State is going to implement it.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e338	‘The Court of Auditors cannot confirm that the 2008 accounts of the State of Labour and Welfare do not contain material errors and deficiencies.’ Weaknesses in internal control create uncertainty about the quality of the accounts. This is, to put it mildly, objectionable, as the accounts of the Employment and Welfare Act constitute one third of the State budget. 2008 was a demanding year for the establishment. It was a huge volume of reform. The growth in unemployment, especially at the end of the year – due to the financial crisis – created an extra heavy labour pressure. The internal control was obviously not adequately prioritised. The hearings confirmed that more attention is now being paid to procedures for and compliance with internal control. It must be possible to expect immediate implementation of measures to ensure sound operational management and an internal audit of satisfactory quality. We cannot live with the fact that one third of the government budget’s expenditure – perhaps 300 billion – cannot be confirmed. One of the intentions of this reform was to make it easier for the user – one door, one case handler and a shorter time to deal with it. Unfortunately, the reality of some Nav offices has often been the opposite. By the fact that the generalist competence has not been in place in the first line service, the user has become a throwball in the system. The establishment of administrative seats has diverted far too much competence from the first line service. Therefore, I am pleased that the Council of State could confirm during the hearing that a thorough review of the current division of labour between the Nav offices and the administrative units has been initiated. Personally, I think the report will show that many of the tasks, and also part of the decision-making authorities, need to be returned to the local Nav offices. Good ICT solutions are essential today for public institutions to carry out their tasks. In Nav, outdated ICT solutions have made work less effective. Different systems in the various fields of activity have made interaction difficult. I believe that good common ICT solutions should be a much higher priority, and that they should be in place in advance of the reform. Another major challenge has been the exchange of competences and skills. It looks like you've had too much faith that the exchange of skills would go on your own, just that you were co-located in the same house. Part of the competence disappeared from the administrative units and there has not been enough time for empowerment in an extremely hectic everyday life. Afterwards, skills plans have been put in place in all Nav offices and the debt-to-budget exchange has given better generalist competence in the first line service. In retrospect, we also see that skills enhancement should have had a greater place in the run-up to the reform, and not just in the run-up. The National Audit Office’s 2008 report must in no way be seen as an evaluation of the Nav reform. There were many Nav offices around the country that were barely established when this report was included in the pen. Only in a few years can we establish how successful this reform has been. Despite several entry problems, with major consequences for some users, I have great faith that the reform will succeed. I notice, among other things. the Joint Organisation of Disabilities, in its opinion, underlines that they have been, and are still, very positive to the Nav reform. Now the situation is also very different around the country. Particularly in Hordaland and Oslo, there have been major problems with residues and unacceptable long waiting time for example. to clarify unemployment benefits, but many offices work well. The report shows that Nav offices in the small municipalities have less waiting time, better personal follow-up and much greater user satisfaction than the larger offices – despite the fact that the financial crisis has also led to an increase in the volume of work, and despite the fact that critical voices were also felt in advance of this reform that the coercion of municipalities was a prerequisite for its functioning in District Norway. Finally, I hope that many of the challenges identified by the report can be included in the work on the Cooperation Reform. Fortunately, the cooperation reform has been built up in such a way that it can be gradually introduced. The community health service must be developed gradually. Equally full, there are many of the same challenges and many parallels to see here. It will be essential to have ICT solutions in place in advance. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e389	I would also like to begin by commending the case rapporteur for a very thorough and very open treatment. In the report submitted by the Riksrevision, it is possible to look at the matter from two different perspectives. One perspective is the need for a state to control its use of money. In this respect, the conclusion contained in the Riksrevision report is very serious, because it is not possible to approve such a large proportion of our state budget and the use of money. I want to focus on the other perspective. It's about what Nav is supposed to be. It is not a forum for dealing with shipowners' taxes or an organised entity to help those who have the most resources to set up. This is the security network in our society. That's what's going to come into force when everything goes wrong. When the world rages around us, it's Nav that's supposed to be the security network. What has been most striking in working on this matter is exactly what Marit Nybakk made, all the stories we've got from users when they've noticed that we've had this debate in Stortinget. That's the other perspective, the citizen's focus, the idea that this should be a system designed for those who have experienced that society is really raging around them, that the entire security network is going on. Then to be told, after the months you have the worst advice, that you should wait six weeks for a refund, or the times you feel that the whole family structure around you is faltering, that it is when you have to have the most energy to go on to get an overview and to find out, I really think is one of the major challenges we face as a society. We're creating a security network that requires so much resources to find out that those who really need help don't get it, while those who are really concerned about finding all sorts of loopholes, or getting involved in all sorts of rules and facing case handlers – knowing and knowing and knowing – can probably be nice on our security network. I think that, for example. The overview of the number of outlying residents in Oslo or other cities is a very good proof of how our system has worked. Because there's no evidence that we don't have a welfare state, there's no evidence that we don't have systems that make everyone have a place to live, but it's proof that we have a system that's so advanced, so little focus on the individual citizen who comes in with his problem, that you can't help those who really, really need help. I've talked to Nav staff who say you're going to type in some indicators on a PC, and then you kind of get the conclusion. I would have wanted a hub that looked much more so to the individual citizen who was sitting there, who had much greater authority locally to see what the individual needs, and also to see what the individual does not need, who could have had a much greater citizen focus in his planning. I think that the challenge for the whole Nav, yes for the whole base of our welfare state in the future, is that we might have to give up some of the millimeters of justice, because the millimeters of justice creates meter injustice for those who fall outside. So maybe we should have simplified our systems. Maybe we should have put a lot more effort into sitting down and seeing what the individual needs right there and then, instead of creating even more advanced systems that make it even harder to get through. Some of the stories I've been given have been really heartbreaking to read. It's about people who, in a phase of life where they get a real break, perceive that they're also going to fight against a system. I have got stories from old ladies who have called and asked questions about shipping and sick transport, and who have been peeled out on the phone and got thrown on the pipe, because they have nothing to ask about it. There must be systems that provide feedback, so that feedback can also be given to employees. Everyone understands that when you work in systems where you also meet people at the extreme edge, it can be demanding, and it can also lead to you moving on the extreme edge. But then there have to be systems that allow it to be picked up, and it can be as simple as maybe some people sometimes have to pick up the phone to call and apologize. When the state is going to make big changes, the state has a big drawback in relation to when private people make big changes, and it's that the government always thinks that they should save on the change. You think if you give too much room for action in the conversion, you'll never be able to pull it in afterwards. This is a finance ministry logic that has been demanding in many major changes in government. That's our job, because we're people's agents. It is we who have the ombudsman role, and we are the ones who have to take care of it. That's why when our citizens, when they're our agents, get bored with the system, it's our job to hear their story, and it's our job to do something about it. I'm afraid that by introducing an agent, you're depoliticating the entire welfare debate. We'll probably get a better everyday life by not being able to relate to people being bored by the system. But I think we have a great deal of good to hear those stories. It's our job to do it too. When it comes to the conversion of Nav, I think I'm going to repeat something that I've said before, and that it's important to remember for state councils that are set to implement and not to initiate. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e429	The attitude of the Control and Constitutional Committee refers, as I see it, to two different themes. One relates to the fact that the Employment and Welfare Act has received an audit letter with comments and that the Riksrevision cannot confirm that there are no errors and shortcomings in the 2008 accounts of the Agency. The second refers to the implementation of the Nav reform and what the Committee expects from the labour and welfare management. In my post I would like to say something about how I follow up both relationships. I share the Committee's view that it is very serious that the Riksrevision cannot confirm the accounts of the Employment and Welfare Act. The criticism of the Riksrevision goes on different aspects. What, in my view, is most serious in the criticism is the weak internal control of the performance management. However, I expect the National Audit Office to be able to find an improvement in the situation in the Labour and Welfare Act through 2009, even though I am prepared for the Riksrevision in the 2009 audit to come up with criticism. In order to improve the level of control in the performance management, the Directorate of Labour and Welfare has put in place key checks for the areas of health benefits, disability benefits, child welfare, child support and social welfare for lone dependants. The checks were carried out in all counties in October 2009 and are followed up by systematic reporting and internal compliance audits. Key checks shall be gradually extended. During the autumn of 2009, the Directorate has developed a comprehensive control strategy for 2009-2015. The aim is to establish over time a level of control based on a risk and materiality assessment to ensure a balance between efficiency and safety/quality requirements in the procedure. In the 2010 letter of award, the Ministry demands that the Employment and Welfare Act be able to refer to improved internal control in the performance management in 2010 compared with 2009 and that the impact of the internal control measures in place be made visible in order to improve the quality of the performance management. Work on strengthening internal control in the Labour and Welfare Act is a long-term work, and it will take time for the establishment to reach the desired level of control. It is also related to today's ICT systems in the building. The development of more fully-valued and integrated ICT solutions remains. It is important that further developments in this area are carried out with good governance and control, and that careful planning is carried out in this area. I would like to inform the Council of the developments in this matter in the future budgetary procedures. The Employment and Welfare Act has had a very complex organisation of the accounting functions based on the organisation of the earlier stages. The Directorate is now uniting the range of accounts into a separate guideline. This will contribute to more effective governance and a clearer sharing of responsibilities in order to improve the quality of the budget's accounts. The Committee also mentions issues related to the implementation of the Nav reform itself. I have now set up an expert group to look at the division of labour between the Nav office and administrative units. The expert group will also be involved from and have dialogue with a reference group composed of representatives of users and employees. The group shall have a two-part delivery. I will inform you in the revised national budget about the group's first delivery of current worksharing and its experience. Based on the group’s final report, which will be available by the end of June, and after the report has been consulted, I will return to the follow-up to the final report in the 2011 budget proposal. Both the National Audit Office and the Committee have been concerned with the need to enhance their competence in the management of labour and welfare. I agree that this is a very central prerequisite for the administration to be able to solve its tasks. These are various measures to increase the competence of those who are now employed in the administration, and at least as much as I stressed in my statement during the committee hearing, that labour and welfare management will be recruiting skilled labour in the future. I have now received the external investigation on long-term competence challenges in relation to the provision of higher education. The investigation will be sent to the hearing in the first instance. In the further work on this matter, I would like to have close contact with the Minister of Research and Higher Education, and see the follow-up in the context of the work currently under way with an action plan to strengthen relations between research, training and practices in the welfare sector. The Committee considers that the target management of the State of Labour and Welfare could be clearer. For 2010, clear requirements have been set for the priority of the establishment. It was made clear in the 2010 budget proposal, it is passed on in the letter of allocation to the budget and characterises the overall administration of the budget. One of my most important tasks in the future is to ensure that new instruments and legislative changes are introduced in such a way that they support the efforts to achieve the overall objectives of the Nav reform and do not create new problems for the agency. Finally, I would also like to comment on Innst. 142 S for 2009–2010 on Nav’s work for an inclusive working life through work centres and the organisation grant. The Employment Centres in the Labour and Welfare Act are a very important instrument for achieving the objectives of the IA Cooperation and the new IA Agreement. This is also emphasised by the social partners. The role of work centres is made clear in the new IA Agreement. I am committed to finding good solutions on the basis of the desire to ensure the most inclusive working life.

That's why I'm surprised that it's said that we're now moving away from the kindergarten's peculiarity, and towards a pediatric ranking culture. This helps to spread a misconception of the quality of the nursery. We do not propose to introduce compulsory language testing of all children in kindergarten. What we are proposing is to make it compulsory to provide a survey of the child's language at the age of three in kindergarten, of course implicitly that parents have given their consent. That means that all kids should have equal opportunities to get the help they need. A mapping will be performed by observing the kid in everyday activity and game; there will not be a test where one ranks the kids. In order to get a good picture of young people’s language and understanding, it is important that this is done in a natural situation that young people are familiar with. A lot of municipalities have some sort of mapping today. I think that a proper review of how this mapping is going to work will increase the quality and help prevent the test culture that we don't want. 92 pst. by the municipalities, the language of the young is surveyed in their kindergartens. We want to ensure that the mapping tools used have the necessary quality through professional assessment and guidance material. The competence and professional beauty of the pedagogue are decisive for the intelligent use of the tools and in line with the legislation. We can't have a situation where staff without adequate competence use simple and quick tools to categorised the kids. We therefore also consider that the staff should be required to have their competence and that the municipalities should help kindergartens who lack adequate skills. An equivalent high-quality childcare provision has led to good and reliable cooperation between kindergarten and home. The younger the young, the more important it is for both young people and parents that staff have time for daily contact and dialogue about the well-being and experience of their children. Many parents want more involvement in kindergarten. Parents have the right to participate, and the rental in the nursery is responsible for inviting parents to participate actively in the planning and assessment of the contents of the nursery. A good childcare facility must be ensured regardless of whether a person is in a private or public nursery, whether it is large or small kindergartens, old or new. Legislation on the equivalent treatment of approved kindergartens will help prevent differences in quality between municipal and non-municipal kindergartens from developing. With this big message, the Government indicates the ambition to ensure a high-quality childcare facility for all. The large majority of young people aged one to five years spend a lot of time in kindergarten. So kindergarten has an important role to play in society and a great deal of co-responsibility for young people getting a safe and healthy upbringing and a basis for a good life.

We see clear signals about a kindergarten that takes steps towards an enterprise more on adults' premises than on the kids' premises. The Christian People's Party therefore promotes a proposal in which we ask the Government not to impose requirements on all kindergartens to offer language mapping at the age of three. The Christian People's Party is also, in part for the same reason, critical to transfer the written assessment of all children from kindergarten to school, from a volunteer to a mandatory pedagogical offer. What's positive is that there's fewer and fewer parents who do it, and hopefully, in a year's time, all parents don't want to go to the stage that you have to pray on your knees to have a kindergarten, because you actually have a full kindergarten. So we've gone from the fact that kindergarten was a right for the few, that it's now become a right for everyone. In 1975, it was actually just 7 pst. of children between one and five years of age with kindergarten, whereas today 96 pst. of all children who begin school have been in kindergarten before they begin school. So this is the biggest welfare reform in 30 years. We have received full child care coverage, and in addition we have legalised the right to children's playground. The message now is that we're taking the next step. Now we've put the quantity in place, and now we're going to ensure the quality further. What's positive here is that despite the massive expansion we've had in recent years, we've managed to keep up the pedagogical density. Well, notice is that some municipalities have received a derogation, but there have been no negative developments. But if we're going to succeed in filling up the unoccupied pre-school classrooms, we're going to have to train many new pre-school teachers in the next few years. That's what the government's been doing. We have expanded the education capacity by 900 new pre-school teacher training places, and if we continue to do so over the next few years, we will be able to cover up the unmanned places in 2012. Still, we'll be far from being in the goal. We – as the rapporteur on the matter mentioned earlier today – are the only OECD country with neither 50 pst. pedagogical density or 80 pst. pediatric competence in kindergartens. So we have to keep educating more. Gradually, we also have to set more ambitious targets for babysitting. I'm a little surprised at the right-hand criticism of kindergartens as a social bias target. On the part of the government parties, we believe that socializing is important, because that's what will give all children a good opportunity, and we know that the children who have gone to kindergarten have better conditions to cope later than children who haven't gone to kindergarten. We also know that children of wealthy parents use kindergarten to a greater extent than children of non-resourced parents. Ergo: Kindergarten is an important tool for equalising social differences. It pointed out the Distribution Committee when they handed over their position to former Minister of Finance Kristin Halvorsen. But this has also been pointed out through the Public Health Institute's research report. In the end, I have to say that I am also a little surprised by the great battle around language mapping, because it is presented as if there is something completely new to start with in kindergartens, and on which all pre-school teachers are contradictingly torn. But 90 pst. by the municipalities today, and there are many pre-school teachers I have met recently, who believe that this is a very positive measure to enable them to identify which children need extra help with their language. They say very clearly that if we're going to make it, we're going to have to check all the kids, so we don't ignore the kids that most need it.

It can help to make the collections and facilities of the museums even more exciting by providing new knowledge through collaboration with other disciplines. It, together with the use of new technologies, can create new ways to disseminate the exciting and diverse content found in our museums. The majority of the Committee also believe that such a commitment can provide opportunities for new exciting technology and business development in the museum field. I said in my introduction that the Norwegian museum landscape may seem inconclusive. The reason I said that is because there are many museums under other departments, which are therefore not covered by the message we are dealing with today, but which are nevertheless an important part of Museums-Norway. In order to improve the coordination of museum and cultural memory policies in this country, the Committee believes that interministerial cooperation between the departments that have museums in their portfolio should be provided. The majority therefore ask the Government to come back to Stortinget with a proposal on how it can be done. The flame of the subject, the flame of history and the burning commitment that has been seen in the case of a lot of museums, is what has raised the Norwegian museum movement, and we can't get such big units that we lose the burning commitment of both volunteers and professionals. It shows what the museum landscape looks like eight years after the reform. These include: the implementation of a major structural reform. Extensive consolidation has led to larger trade unions and has strengthened the museums, so that they have more resources to play at. This will be a work that we will strengthen towards the 200th anniversary.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e1707	I would like to thank the Committee for its work on this matter. This is an important theme which the Progress Party has also put on the agenda on various occasions. As rapporteur of the case, I wanted to elaborate on the subject of a worthy death. Unfortunately, we get a polarization fast when we are going to debate death and not least a worthy death. We all know that death is the price we humans have to pay for life, yet death is taboo and, through constant institutionalization, largely removed from people's everyday lives. That is why I am pleased that the Committee is united in a number of joint comments on both quality of life and autonomy in relation to the dying. It was essential for me to emphasise the need for an overall approach to the dying and its relatives, and stress that this goes on at different levels to ensure that death can become a natural continuation of life. The Committee is broadly in favour of such emphasis. Proposals want a life-help plan to be drawn up and five points should be highlighted. The Party of Progress and the Right share such a vision, with the exception of the form of financing for mitigating treatment. The government parties do not want a life support plan, but point through our common comments to several improvement points in view of serious sick and dying patients. There is a cross-political consensus on the importance of strengthening continuing training and research efforts in the area of relieving treatment. It also points to the need for cooperation both horizontally and vertically in healthcare. The Committee believes that it is necessary to stimulate the establishment of outpatient teams and that there is a need to look at what prevents such teams from being set up to an adequate extent today. An overall committee points out that there are too few municipal hospitals today in relation to the need. It also refers to the fact that most health businesses have palliative teams and the importance of ensuring the transfer of competences from these teams to other departments and environments. Hospices have been groundbreaking in terms of giving the dying a worthy end to life through a holistic approach of good and caring care and, not least, humanity. This is a form of palliative treatment that may include pain relief, mental follow-up and overall care. Serious illness, death, and grief can jeopardize one's self-image, and can be experienced as a personal threat. Fear, fear, helplessness, despair and anger are normal feelings in such a situation. But death need not be evil and painful for a human being. For some, death can actually be a relief that is met with relief and tranquillity. The palliative treatment aims to meet seriously ill and dying and their relatives in a holistic way that takes account of all these factors. There is a significant bias over who is receiving a palliative offer today. Crushed amounts to 95 pst. by patients on palliative units. It is important to focus also on other groups of seriously ill and dying patients, so that they also receive a comprehensive treatment. All 95 pst. by those who die in nursing homes do not receive the adequate relief and care they need in the final phase of life. I also chose to raise seriously ill and dying children in this case, and sees to my delight that it has caused several others to focus on the issues of parents of children born with severe malformations and limited life prospects. Annually, about 160 children die in their homes without the child or relatives having any professional network around them. It is almost a contradiction that at the beginning of life children will die. But it happens, unfortunately. Some parents do not deal with such a situation at all. I have heard of parents who have depreciated their child in the first year of life and avoided contact, while the child has been subject to public care for nearly 20 years. I have heard of parents who have gone fully into the task of providing the child with the greatest possible care and love, giving up their own job and career while they have been living with some sort of waiting care. I expect the Council of State to take a closer look at this issue. There is a great need to increase the effort to help the seriously ill and dying and their relatives. The funding of relieving treatment is a key factor here. I would strongly recommend to the government parties to look at proposal No. Four more times. In an explanatory form, the parties to the government agree to this proposal. The only thing this proposal has in addition is to ask for financial barriers to interaction to be removed. It will be a bad signal in view of the work of the Cooperation Reform, whether that is what makes the Government parties not to support the proposal. The Progress Party points out that today’s funding key such as the ‘Bondevik parties’ and the red-greens is behind, can be said to premise the hospitals in the event of short deathbeds, which we find suspicious. Funding must be organised in such a way that the dying person is treated in a dignified way, even if the death phase is prolonged. Pain is central when we talk about relieving treatment. It is therefore incomprehensible to me that the Progress Party is standing alone talking about patients who experience pain relief no longer having an effect. When even medical associations in Scandinavia have studies on how doctors will handle patients with uncontrolled pain, politicians cannot close their eyes. It is precisely as the ethical and medical dilemma of large doses of painkillers that can actually take life arises. This has nothing to do with active death help, but is the reality of daily health professionals, and who also politicians have to dry up to address. We can't just wait for a new Sandsdalen, Ottesen or Husebø case and then act on the basis of tabloid suggestions. Medical guidelines and guides are needed, there is a need for a plan of overall care and care for the ill and dying and their relatives – regardless of the age of the deceased and whether the deceased is at home or in an institution. I take this up with me the proposals to which the Committee on the Environment, Public Health and Consumer Protection is advocating.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e1729	Life help and relief in the final phase of life is one thing that affects us all. We are concerned that the individual himself should be able to choose where to end your life as far as possible. Today, most people die in hospitals or hospitals, but the possibility of ending their life in a familiar environment with their family and loved ones around them can be a real alternative for many. That is why we are concerned about having good knowledge and good practice for palliative treatment in municipalities. We see that further development of palliative sites in the community health service should become an important part of the cooperation reform – building out outpatient palliative teams and places for relief. A great deal has been done in the area of life support and relief, and in the 2015 care plan there are several measures to strengthen the provision of services to care users, including those in the final phase of life. There is currently a grant scheme for relief and care at the end of life aimed at strengthening the provision of facilities outside the hospital. The grant is intended for community health services and some measures directed by private and non-profit institutions serving households. Furthermore, the grant for empowerment measures for relief and care at the end of life has been collected under the same heading as grants to teaching homes and teaching homes. The measures shall be seen in the context of national cancer strategy, where the competence and capacity to follow-up of patients in the municipal service are central, and they must be seen in the context of the activity of educational hospitals. With regard to the competence of nursing staff, we will refer to the Government's competence and recruitment plan The Competence Plan 2015, which has as its objective, among other things. raising the formal level of education in the care service, creating greater professional scope and strengthening guidance, in-house training and continuing training. An important subject area for the lift of competences is precisely palliation. As part of the commitment to competence, a project has been launched to enhance ethical competence in the service. The ‘Cooperation on Ethical Competences’ project shall contribute to enhancing the ethical competence of municipal health and care services. In 2009, 100 municipalities participated in the project. Over time, all municipalities shall be given the opportunity to participate in the project and to establish meeting places for ethical reflection. A lot of good palliative treatment is taking place today at hospitals, in the municipalities and at the home of the individual, but we can always get better, and we have to work for that.
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e1742	I will first give tribute to the Christian People's Party which has proposed a plan for life support. Right-wing support for all proposals except for proposal No. 6, concerning financing. In addition, I would like to thank the rapporteur and congratulate him for a thorough and good work on the matter, as well as for the presentation here in the Chamber. Care at the end of life is perhaps one of the most demanding health care tasks that challenges us both as professionals and politicians. Every day, people experience serious illness and close losses that force them into a totally changed life situation. We meet such challenges very differently, depending on how prepared we are, the life situation we are in, reactions in the environment, and not least the help provided from the health and care services. Respect for human dignity is put on a serious test when we meet people in the final phase of life. It is in these situations that the real human vision of healthcare and health professionals comes for one day. It's just close to the extremes we find the value test pieces in our society. Human dignity is especially vulnerable to being violated or threatened in the final phase of life. Death is institutionalised, decriminalised and largely left to specialists. Although the offer has been much better in recent years, there are still far too many examples of serious ill and dying getting inadequate help. In order to counteract the demands for health care to contribute to the self-chosen end of life, it is important to ensure that the offer given to people at the end of life is as good as possible both human and professional. The main challenge is to ensure that the individual patient is properly offered, of good quality and at the right level of healthcare. As far as possible, provision must be made for healthcare to be provided as close as possible to home or at home if the patient so wishes. A serious illness affects not only the patient but also the immediate family, friends and community. Many seriously ill patients are often isolated, they are not seeking contact themselves, and those on the outside are afraid to care. The specialisation in the health care system means that you can lose the whole of your care. This is where the hospice philosophy has shown its way. The value base is optimal treatment, care and care that addresses the physical, mental, social and spiritual and existential needs of patients. Good and thoughtful offers create trust and security for patients, for relatives and for professionals. The main point is that people are met and seen. It is necessary to give serious sick and dying a real choice in relation to where they want to spend the last days of life. This is particularly important in the light of the fact that we are a multicultural society, where people have different views on how a worthy end of life should be. For many, it's important to be at home in the final phase of life, others want to be somewhere else where they can have peace. This requires that we provide good home-based care and at the same time build several hospices as an offer to those who want it. If more people are to be given the opportunity to end their lives at home in accordance with their own wishes, we must provide them and their relatives with security for qualified assistance. Ambulatory palliative teams can provide this security, provided that they have flexible arrangements in the municipal health and specialist health services, and that they cooperate and interact on a comprehensive offer. Private offers funded by the public can be an important resource in the provision of services to the seriously ill and dying. Such offers must be adapted to the ordinary service and be part of the total offer to the individual patient. The words ‘unfortunately, there is nothing more we can do’ are often heard from health professionals when someone is told that curative treatment does not help. Then I would say, well, it's decided "more" we can do, but this "more" is something other than an attempt to heal. This ‘more’ is about helping a human being live until that door.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e1807	The representatives of Davøy, Høybräten and Syversen have suggested that the Government prepare a life support plan. When input-driven financing is used as an income system at a departmental level, each department experiences the same economic compensation as the length of time it lasts. A financing mechanism that premises short rents is ethically very questionable. Patients with advanced disease may have periods of disease with very complex challenges that require long-term hospitalisation. The Central Party therefore looks forward to a review of the financing system by the Government and welcomes the proposal to move away from the ISF and to full funding of the palliative offer through the framework. The funding of palliative hospital beds, palliative teams and palliative services from the permanent staff also needs a review. I expect this to be assessed in the light of the interaction reform and the forthcoming initiative on the municipal health service. In the Government's Competence and Recruitment Plan, the Competence Plan 2015, palliation is an important subject area. The government's new laws will make it easier to care for close relatives. I would like to show that a new ten-day leave for employees is introduced to provide the necessary care for parents and spouses/partners. It also extends the right to leave and care allowance in the care of close relatives in the final phase of life, from 20 to 60 days. Teaching homes and teaching homes are other measures to help improve the quality of care services, by focusing on research, professional development and higher levels of competence in the service. This will be important measures to ensure the palliative competence of the community health services in general and of hospitals in particular.

On this basis, I do not wish to support a specific life support plan, as the measures outlined in the proposal are to be carried out by the ordinary municipal services and measures already taken. ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2065	Thanks to the case-rapporteur for a good job and a thorough presentation of his proposal for changes to the Special Health Service Act in order to allow the approval of institutions that provide interdisciplinary specialised treatment in hospitals and are therefore subject to free hospital choice. The starting point of the proposal is that patients with eating disorders do not receive adequate public health care assistance. There are long waiting lists for treatment in several places. Patients who require daily treatment for their eating disorders are seriously ill, and it is unsustainable that they have to wait for weeks and months for help. We have received several enquiries from the desperate people with eating disorders and their relatives. They tell us about serious health problems and inadequate follow-up from healthcare. Several have pointed out that they have received very good help from Capio Anorexia Senter, an offer many have had to pay for themselves. Although Capio has a professionally recognised offer with which patients are satisfied, many people feel that they are not allowed to use the offer for public account. Therefore, the right wants to amend the law to ensure that good interdisciplinary health institutions that meet professional requirements to be part of the specialist healthcare service can also be recognised as hospitals, although they do not have a clear somatic approach, are an offer to drug addicts or a district psychiatric centre. We believe that this will ensure a better offer adapted to the individual patient, in particular patient groups that today have a lack of offer. We believe it is unacceptable that bureaucratic barriers prevent patients from using an offer that can provide them with rapid help. The Soria Moria declaration and mission documents to regional health establishments indicate that the use of private commercial offers should be restricted. The justification seems to be a purely ideological defence of the public service monopoly. Now, when we register a certain attendance from the government parties in this matter, I believe it is clear evidence that this monopoly policy does not face reality. A policy where good private offerings are only available to those who have good advice is not in contact with individuals, especially when health cows and waiting times only increase. It leads to a classed health care system, which is not acceptable to the right. On the right, private services contribute to the quality and diversity of public health services. Many individuals have been pioneers in professional development and resource use. The private provision improves public health and also increases patients’ freedom of choice. We believe that patients from all over the country must avail themselves of the offer at Capio Anorexia Centers on behalf of the state by virtue of the right to free hospital choice. That is why we have promoted this legislative amendment. As with drug addicts, people with eating disorders will need a wide and diverse range of services. The request by the Minister of Health and Health for health businesses to use the available capacity of Capio has not been made. When Stortinget discussed a proposal to include Capio in free hospital elections last year, the government parties stated that this requires a change in law. The Minister of Health and Health now says that no legislative change is required to authorise Capio Anorexia Centres and other similar services as hospitals, so that they can be included in the right to free hospital choice. Therefore, there must be a political justification for refusing to accept the offer. In 2009, the Ministry of Health and Health approved the establishment of the hospital Norsk Toppheelsenter, which is operated by Norsk Toppfotball. The offer shall provide treatment to sportsmen with eating disorders. The right considers that patients with eating disorders but who are not top athletes must also have the opportunity to use private treatment institutions. Therefore, the right considers that the conditions for the approval of hospitals should allow for the approval of multi-disciplinary specialised health services that do not operate hospitals of a purely somatic nature. This, in our view, will contribute to a greater diversity of treatment provision, increased freedom of choice for patients and not least shorter waiting time. I take up the proposal from the Right and Christian People's Party, which is promoted in the position.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2089	It is the specialist services and, therefore, the regional health professionals, through their caring for responsibilities, who are currently responsible for the necessary health care, distribution and treatment of patients with eating problems. This is regulated by the law of the specialist services. In this respect, the proposers propose to amend the law on specialist services, to allow for the recognition of institutions providing multi-disciplinary specialised treatment. They argued that such a change in law would lead to inclusion in the free choice of hospitals. According to the proposers, patients from all health regions can benefit from multi-disciplinary specialised treatment, even if the supply exists only in some of the regions. The proposers claim that such a change would lead to better health provision in Norway. They also claim that the Government does not want to use the free capacity of private actors for ideological reasons. The centre party disagrees with the proposers in many of the leaders being set up, but we are some in that the offer given by Capio Anorexia Centers is a good addition to the public health service, and patients across the country can benefit from it. We think it's important that there should be a lack of supply that is given. Health care providers must then benefit from the existing services where they themselves do not have the corresponding services to provide. The specific treatment provision of regional healthcare providers is already part of the free choice of hospitals. With today’s legislation, there are no formal obstacles to regional healthcare providers’ well-management of their statutory tasks through agreements with private service providers, such as Capio Anorexia Centers. Regional health professionals must at any time assess whether the supply to people with eating disabilities is of sufficient quality and capacity, and evaluate the need to use private activities to increase capacity. So the center party cannot see that a change in the law is necessary if we are to get a better health care service in Norway, as the proposers write in the setting. Nor is it the case that a change in the law will automatically lead to inclusion in the free choice of hospitals, as suggested by the proposers. In all cases, it is an obligation for private institutions to have an agreement with a regional health agency to be included in the free choice of hospitals. In assessing whether hospital approval is to be granted, the Department may, inter alia: stress, as the Minister of Health and Health writes, social or professional concerns about the approval of the institution, whether the institution is covered by plans drawn up by the regional health agency, and whether the services to be provided appear to be defenceable. That's how we still think it should be. The intentions of the law must not be eroded by granting exemptions from and facilitating hospital requirements in order to be part of the free hospital choice. The centre party believes that we must accept that it is the regional health professionals today who have been given responsibility for the provision of specialist health services in their region, as well as the responsibility for the distribution and treatment of eating problems. In particular, the parties which were the driving force behind a regulation and a regionalisation of the specialist services must soon be ‘committed’ to this.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2121	The rapporteur was very concerned that this was about dealing with individuals. I fully agree with that. These are individuals, and for this very reason, in order to ensure a good health service for all, we need a strong public health service – where hospitals are essentially owned and operated by the public. This capacity must also be used. At the same time, the public must at all times assess whether the offer is of sufficient quality and capacity and use private activities and contract specialists where it is necessary to supplement the offer ad hoc or on a fixed basis. I do not see reason to implement a change in the law that will not lead to an appropriate change in the legal situation. I therefore agree with the majority's conclusion that the draft representative is annexed to the Minutes. The Specialist Health Service Act regulates the responsibilities of regional health establishments for the specialist health service. This includes the responsibility for the necessary health care for patients with eating disorders and the necessary health care for patients with drug abuse. This applies even if the term ‘other multidisciplinary specialised treatment’ is not mentioned in the law. Against this background, it is not necessary to amend the Section 2-1a of the Special Health Service Act, as these areas are already considered to be part of the specialist health service. It is the department that approves hospitals based in the specialist health service law § 4-1. The hospital approval requirement applies to both somatic and psychiatric hospitals. Hospitals are not defined by law, but the pre-worker of the law assumes that hospital services include medical examination and treatment activities that are organised to keep the patient overnight where this is desirable or necessary for medical reasons. When considering whether approval may be granted, the following shall be taken into account: whether social or professional reasons justify the institution's recognition of whether the institution is covered by plans drawn up by regional health undertakings concerning the services to be provided appears to be sound. A comprehensive discretionary and comprehensive assessment is required to determine whether the intentions of the law are being fulfilled. When, after today's legal situation, it is not considered appropriate to approve an establishment as a hospital, I see no reason to relax on these requirements in order to allow the establishment to become part of the free hospital choice system. In any case, it is not sufficient to be part of the specialist healthcare service in order to be eligible for the free choice of hospitals under the Patient Rights Act § 2-4. In order to be eligible for the scheme, private institutions must have contracts with a regional health undertaking. The proposal will therefore not mean any practical change for patients with eating disorders within the region and is not, in my view, suitable for achieving the intentions of the proposers. Regional health establishments are responsible for the investigation and treatment of these patient groups by the specialist healthcare service. The health care provided from the public hospitals and outpatient clinics and from private hospitals by agreement is already subject to free hospital choice. With regard to this patient group, it is important to stress that regional health companies have been informed to establish a health offer for this type of patient, and that has also been done in all regional health establishments. In any case, with current legislation, there are no formal obstacles to regional health businesses choosing to perform their statutory tasks through agreements with private service providers, such as: Capio Anorexia Senter.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2214	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. 7th@item: inlistbox [[Fotation, see page 1979]]
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2230	Today, the majority has a proposal to investigate the use of the medicinal product Naloxon nasal spray as part of the management of overdose deaths. The proposers suggest that Norway is in the world peak in the number of reported overdose deaths relative to the population. This is consistent with data from the 2008 Annual Report on the state of the drugs problem in Europe. There are different registration practices in the countries covered by the statistics and a recently published article in the Norwegian Medical Association Journal No. 21 in 2009 shows that overdose statistics in Norway include more than heroin-related deaths. Nevertheless, there is no doubt that overdose deaths are too high and that all good measures should be taken to reduce them. The acute treatment of heroin and similar substances overdoses is the fastest possible injection of the medicinal product Naloxone. This is an antidote, and Naloxone is authorised in Norway for use in blood or muscle injections. It has long been a possible strategy that the antidote should be available in or near the user environment to reduce the risk of overdose deaths. There has been some resistance to putting this into use, both for practical reasons and because of the risk of transmission at times. A nasal spray has therefore been developed, and by this form of administration, the antidote is absorbed into the blood and brought into cells in the brain to counteract the effect of heroin overdose. According to the Health Directorate, small studies have been conducted comparing the use of nasal spray with injection, but safe conclusions are not yet available. It is hoped that simple use of a nasal spray may be an alternative to current treatment with injecting. The next step must be to show that the measure is effective in reducing overdose deaths. Each overdose death is one too many. Therefore, new proposals shall be carefully considered and followed up and, as indicated in the letter of 18 December 2009 to the Health and Welfare Committee, the Council will ask the Health Directorate to consider whether attempts to use Naloxon nasal spray should be initiated to prevent overdose deaths. The acute treatment of overdoses is one of many elements of a worthy care for drug users. I welcome the fact that this proposal receives a thorough medical-professional assessment and looks forward to learning about the results. The Committee recommends that the draft representative be annexed to the minutes.
