ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2357	Psychiatric disorders are a people's disease. Several international studies show that about 50 pst. have a mental illness during life, and 20-30 pst. has had such a disorder in the last year. Most of us will experience mental problems in some form of life. The World Health Organisation assumes that the overall burden of disability and premature death due to depression will increase, and in 2020 mental disorders will be the main cause of disease stress in Western countries. When the Stortinge dealt with the public health report in 1997, the diagnosis had failed at all stages. The Roadmap for Mental Health was launched in 1998 and ended in 2008. The plan was clear on direction and quantitative targets. There was to be a better and more decentralised provision, and outpatient services in the patient’s community were to replace institutional care. The evaluation report of the Norwegian Research Council shows that the quantitative targets of the escalation plan have been far-reaching. Some examples illustrate this: There are more than twice as many outpatient treatments as at the time of initiation of the escalation plan. It is built 75 new district psychiatric centers, which have given many patients an offer where they live. There are about 3,400 more well-organized housing for the mentally ill in the municipalities. Although many of the quantitative objectives of the escalation plan have been achieved, we do not aim to provide a good and equivalent treatment provision to the mentally ill, for example.\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t There are still too long waiting times and too many patient rights violations. There is too much pressure on the daily treatment offer because there is a lack of good locally available information. There is a lack of coherence and overallity in services, which affects all patients, but psychologically ill suffers particularly hard. The user involvement and the related perspective need to be addressed better. And not least, there has to be help that actually helps. This interpellation is about the latest challenge, namely that mental illness should be assisted. After making great efforts to strengthen the quantity of mental health protection, we now need to work more to improve quality. An illustrative example of not always helping is from the offer to patients with coercive disorders. On November 4, 2009, TV 2 showed the documentary “Psych transformation!” The programme showed that effective treatment is available for patients suffering from coercive disorders but that few patients are offered this. Forced illness is estimated to be the fourth most common mental disorder and affects more than 50,000 Nordics at any time. This is a debilitating disorder that takes a lot of time, and leads to the patient having major problems in everyday life and at work. Many are dissatisfactioned because of suffering, and it usually imposes a heavy burden on relatives as well. Since the 1970s, it has been known that so-called exposure therapy with response prevention is an effective treatment method for the disorder. Through a dozen studies, it has been demonstrated that the method is cost-effective and provides a significant improvement in 60-70 pst. by the patients. The approach is recommended in the Health Supervision Guidelines for Treatment and the district psychiatric centres are asked to have competence in this area. Despite this, there are very many patients who are not given this treatment. In the light of that documentary, I asked what the Council of State would do to ensure that all patients are offered the treatment in question if they have this disorder. The Council of State then took a price-effective initiative to keep a course in this treatment for health businesses. I am aware that the Norwegian Association for Cognitive Therapy is about to implement a better treatment plan for patients with coercive disorders, and will commend professionals in this environment for addressing the matter and thus providing for an improvement in the offer to patients. But this case raises issues that apply to mental health protection in general. How can we ensure that professionals and treatment institutions have sufficient expertise to provide good treatment? What do we do to make sure that the treatment of mentally ill people is based on knowledge-based methods, and not the method that the caregiver at all times seems to be best applied to all disorders, or generally to a wide range of patient groups? A key factor in ensuring good quality in treatment provision is knowledge of treatment and what are effective methods. On the right, we believe that there is too little emphasis on quality work in health enterprises, and we have therefore proposed a new quality strategy for health enterprises. We believe that several clinical guides need to be developed to ensure that knowledge-based and effective methods are used. While it is clearly necessary to exercise clinical judgement, it should not be left to the discretion of the individual handler whether or not knowledge-based practice is used. It must not be that when a patient enters a psychiatric office, one can see who the psychiatrist has a picture on the wall, which treatment method one achieves. I want to challenge the Minister of Health and Care both to develop more treatment guides and to ensure that health businesses actually follow these guidelines. Mild recommendations are not enough. From a political point of view, we must make at least as strict demands on quality as we make for financial management. In order to succeed in quality work, we also need to make better measurements of the quality of mental health protection. Several indicators have been developed in mental health protection, including: for the extent of cleaning, the use of coercion and the number of corridor Asians. We also have several user studies that show that many patients are satisfied with the offer. But we also need good measurements of the outcome of the treatment. We need to know that help helps. The right considers that the development of a decentralised supply to mentally ill people has been correct and necessary. We have to give more mentally ill help where they live, so that they can master life as best they can in their community. Better accessibility is essential for better help for mentally ill people. A key challenge is to ensure better coherence in the supply of sick people. The cooperation reform, which is now under consideration in the Committee, will contribute to improvements in supply in this way. At the same time, the decentralised structure of district psychiatric centres and outpatient teams implies that one must have quite a broad range of competences. Psychiatric disease is complex, and a high level of treatment is required for the caregiver to provide a specialised offer of investigation and treatment to all patients in their application area. The DPSs and the BUPs are at the core. In particular, specialised treatment provision for smaller patient groups is involved. This can help to strengthen professional development and improve the quality of treatment provision. Such developments may thus strengthen the possibilities for patients to receive help as helpers. I would challenge the Council of State to respond to whether we can find a better balance between availability and decentralisation on the one hand and the need for quality and specialisation on the other. The Mental Health Council has produced a report on the quality of mental health protection with seven main elements necessary to ensure good quality in the supply of mental illness. One of these is that the training of professionals must follow a better development. The Council points out that continuing training in mental health work and the specialisation of psychologists and psychiatrists are not adapted to current services and user intervention requirements. Today's service structure requires, as I said, broad competence, which can impose enormous demands on professionals. Psychologists can hardly be expected to provide good exposure therapy to patients with coercive disorders after a four-hour training course. I would encourage the Council of State to review training provision in the sector together with its colleague in the Ministry of Research, in order to improve and update education provision. On behalf of the then Social and Health Directorate, an analysis of the need for mental health protection competence was prepared, which was published in the spring of 2006. The analysis concludes that nearly 500 psychiatrists and a significant number of psychologist specialists are lacking at the district psychiatric centres. National audit survey 2007 showed that treatment provision for children and young people with mental health disorders is not equivalent. There are large differences in capacity and share of specialists between outpatient clinics and between health businesses. It is clear that many other educational groups can offer a very good supply to mentally ill people, and it is not desirable for any professional monopoly in any part of the health sector. However, we need to ensure that there is sufficient professional competence both in the district medical centres and in outpatient clinics for children and young people.

Furthermore, national guidelines have been published or under work in a number of fields of psychiatric treatment. The supervisors recommend the maximum duration of a patient with a given diagnosis waiting for treatment and the type of treatment that should be implemented. Municipalities, regional health enterprises and the Health Directorate cooperate on the introduction of guidance and guidance services. Psychiatric health protection has been strengthened and modernised in the last ten years. We have developed a decentralised mental health system with a total of 75 district psychiatric centres covering all the municipalities of the country. The objective is to provide high-quality and coherent services. Mental health protection with emphasis on decentralised services is in line with developments in other Western countries and with recommendations from the World Health Organisation. The situation at the DPS was reviewed in 2008. On this basis, regional health firms have been given the task of strengthening the transition from institutional treatment to more out-of-court and out-patient services. Furthermore, they shall upgrade the DPSs professionally and manned. The role of hospitals shall be clarified in order to provide access to mental health protection functions. It is important to dimension the distribution of resources between the DPS and the hospital departments in order to adapt the supply to the needs of the population. On the issue of the structure of mental health protection, I would like to stress that the needs of the population are to be taken into account. Education must ensure that education is adapted to the model of local services. This means that the user perspective, mastery, interaction and interdisciplinary approach must be integrated into education. The DPS will be the core experience and knowledge base. The basis for specialist knowledge in psychiatry, psychology and mental health work is laid in specialist training, in continuing and continuing training and through clinical practice. This means that we need to make demands on universities, colleges and other educational institutions, but also on the service as a clinical knowledge base for education. There are strict requirements for practice to become a specialist in psychology or psychiatry, and these requirements cannot be waived. I feel confident that a well-developed network of good DPSs, combined with good hospital services, will be the best basis for future knowledge development. The DPSs are large enough to provide both generalist competence in mental health protection and special skills in areas such as psychosis teams, double diagnosis teams, emergency teams, etc. Health regions need to consider whether it is appropriate to allocate the tasks between the DPSs in the region in order to have an offer for patients with less frequent disorders. Hospitals will provide the clinical knowledge base in their fields. We're still in a construction phase to get full-value DPSs across the country. It is a challenge for clinical provision and competence, but it does not provide a basis for reconsidering the intentions of changing the structure of mental health services. We still need to press on the switch to mental health protection. I would like to stress that there has been a substantial increase in the competence of mental health protection in the escalation plan and afterwards. A number of competence functions have been established, a large number of staff have undergone further training in multidisciplinary mental health work and, in particular, recruitment to the field has been significantly improved. This applies to specialists in psychology and psychiatry as well as university-trained personnel. The Health Directorate has been commissioned to undergo specialist medical training in order to assess the need to change the structure and content. They shall also review the provision of continuing training by private institutions and associations receiving government grants. The questions raised by the representative, Mr Höie, deserve definite attention for a long time in the future. My assessment is that we are on the right track with the political decisions that have been taken and the actions that have been taken.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2550	The evaluation report for the Mental Health Progression Plan shows that ten years with the escalation plan have given a boost to mental health work in Norway. Half of hospital psychiatric hospital emergencies should have taken action at a regional psychiatric/municipal level. 40 pst. the daily hospitalisation of district psychiatric centres should have been available at municipal level. Up to 400,000–500,000 patients before mental health protection are currently treated in psychiatric wards and waiting for housing and care in municipalities. This shows that poor accessibility not only owes to the economy but also to poor organisation and methods of work and lack of cooperation. So the structural work in psychiatry must continue, so that we get more emphasis on prevention, early diagnosis and improvement of the quality of psychiatry services in municipalities in particular. This effort must also have implications for the training of health workers in psychiatry. More have to be explored and further explored with a view to working in the community health service and in the prevention work.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2623	It is true that one has recently had a very high focus on a specific case by one of the healthcare companies related to – at least suspected – a manipulation of the date of convocation to patient studies and treatment. What is the intention of this interpellation is to address the challenge it poses by the fact that a health-care provider has the right to special treatment, to implement the right to specialised treatment and to finance the right to the implementation of specialised treatment. This gives, according to what is also, for example. Professor Syse has addressed a challenge related to the goat and oatmeal problem. The problem that patients’ rights are not adequately respected is documented by a number of recent reports relating to patients’ rights, to which I will return. When the Patient Rights Act was adopted in June 1999, it gave patients the right to assessment, health care and the right to free hospital choice. However, the right to an individual time limit for treatment was proposed by the Bondevik II Government and was adopted in the Stortinget on 2 December 2003. At the same time, a right of treatment was introduced in private establishments in Norway or abroad, if the deadline was broken. In the opinion, the Committee unanimously stated: ‘The Committee has noted that the Ministry proposes that the healthcare should inform the patients concerned of the expiry of the treatment period. The treatment period will have a greater real impact on patients when they are given a concrete time to which they can relate and adjust.’ But the question of this interpellation is simply whether the Storting's intention of patient rights and the Patient Rights Act is fulfilled. Have patients been significantly improved in terms of rights and legal certainty? Report No. of the Court of Auditors 5 for 2008–2009 summarises a study on mental health protection. The press release of the Riksrevision stated: ‘Health professionals practice the prioritisation rule very differently. Obviously, legal spaces are refused, while patients who should have been refused are admitted. There is no link between diagnosis and prioritisation of the patient or between diagnosis and deadline for treatment.» In addition, the report states: ‘The setting of time limits for delayed treatment also appeared to be more marked in terms of capacity than in terms of patient care.’ The Health Directorate’s report on waiting times and waiting lists for the second quarter of 2009 once again pointed out wide variations in the allocation of health care rights: ‘There are large regional variations in rights allocation in somatic disciplines. The share allocated to the right to necessary health care varies from 44 to 82 percent between regions in the second tertiary of 2009. Health Mid-Norway is distinct from the other regions with a higher rate of entitlement.’» A new Health Directorate report related to the 2009 waiting list situation was presented today. This report confirmed exactly the same thing: ‘There are variations in the allocation of rights between different sectors and regions. For the somatic sector, there are wide variations between regions in the proportion of patients given priority. In the third tertiary in 2009, Health Middle Norway gave priority to 80 percent of patients, while Health Nord prioritised 48 percent of patients. In adult mental health protection, the proportion of priority patients varied from 91 per cent to 66 per cent [[Health Mid-Norway]] [[Health North]] Health oversight carried out a two-year supervision of mental health protection in District Psychiatric Centres. In report 4/2009, they provide a summary after one year of supervision, entitled ‘DPS, nationwide services, varying quality?’. The report mentions, among other things:\t\t You can change the settings of the ‘In 15 of the 28 DPS, the assessment and prioritisation of referrals did not always comply with the requirements laid down by law, regulation or administrative action.’; Further: ‘In some DPS, the waiting time was found to be up to six months for the first consultation and treatment periods were set independently of the patient’s condition and situation.’ Further: ‘The health oversight of the Member States is concerned that there still appears to be widespread failure in the prioritisation and evaluation of patients referred to in the DPS. In addition to the fact that this may have serious consequences for the individual patient’s health care, errors and deficiencies in this area may also contribute to weakening the interaction between DPS and reference agencies such as prescribers and other healthcare elements.’ In the Riksrevision report No. 3 for 2009–2010, once again weaknesses were identified in the ownership management of regional health businesses by the Ministry of Health and Welfare. In the press release from the Riksrevision, it is called, among other things,\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t ‘The application of the deadline for the treatment of patients in multidisciplinary specialised treatment for drug addicts in Health South-East could lead to a serious breach of the Patient Rights Act. [[TSB]] Patients are encouraged to accept that the initial treatment period, based on medically sound waiting time, is delayed due to a lack of institution’s capacity. Patients are not provided with sufficient and correct information to give a qualified consent for a limited period of time for patients, thereby enabling them to exercise their rights.’ In the light of the National Audit Office’s report in 2008-2009, the Right proposed to set up an independent assessment body for the patient’s rights already at the time, so that hospitals cannot limit these rights on the basis of their own capacity and economy. The Minister of Health then rejected this problem and said to the Stortinget, in the form of a letter, when the Committee considered the matter: ‘There is no reason to claim that patients are not given a time limit for treatment based on a responsible medical professional assessment. I do not see the need at the moment to make changes to the Patient Rights Act in this area." This interpellation has just arisen in order to have a broader debate on how many reports from the responsible authorities and the supervisory authorities a Health Minister from the Workers' Party needs before admitting that patients' rights are not being sufficiently followed up in our healthcare companies, that they are being manipulated with these rights in view of taking into account capacity considerations, and not taking into account the patient's medical condition, which was the clear premise on the part of Stortinget when adopting the new Patient Rights Act. The case from Asker and Bærum is probably a single event that illustrates that there are very unwise ways to manipulate patient rights that are easy to discover and easy to comply with. The challenge for the Council of State must be to run a system where there are an incredible number of more, significantly more complicated and more difficult ways to control and manipulate the same rights, and that this does not go beyond those patients who themselves have the least opportunity to follow up their rights, who have the least opportunity to follow up that they get a letter in the mailbox saying that if their rights are not followed up, they can contact HELFO, simply because they do not have a mailbox, and simply because they do not read letters. What discussions relating to our health services and our system will the Council of State open up, so that this should be individual rights for the individual, and not rights that safeguard the system in front of the individual?
ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2686	As I read the interpellation, it started with Asker and the Bærum case, and I've also taken that as my starting point. However, as also noted, this case is under police investigation and under medical supervision. Therefore, I do not want to give an assessment of these matters, but just stress that if it turns out that the Hospital Asker and Bærum have changed the date of the deadline for processing in order to save money, I see it very seriously. Patients entitled to the necessary health care should be given a concrete date for the last time they receive treatment in the specialist healthcare system. The patient shall be informed of this period. The deadline is legally binding for specialist healthcare and the patient has a legal claim for treatment within the prescribed period. The aim of the right to necessary health care is to ensure an equivalent level of healthcare for patients across the country and that patients are also given proper priority. We have established a separate contact point, HELFO Patient Service, where the patient can contact if the deadline is not met. Health businesses get a day to find an offer where the patient can receive treatment within 14 days. If they are unable to do so, HELFO will take over and order treatment from an institution with which they have a framework agreement or abroad. The regional health undertaking is responsible for covering the cost. It is likely that it costs more to pay for treatment by others than to use its own capacity. Therefore, health businesses will benefit from the fact that patients do not use their right to receive treatment elsewhere. The representative, Höie, states that the order and delivery roles are not sufficiently separate from the current system, and states that this is a possible reason why hospitals can use different methods of shifting the timing of deadlines. The model of health enterprises is to a certain extent based on a distinction between the functions of ordering and performing by dividing the roles of the regional health enterprise on the one hand, and health enterprises and other healthcare providers on the other. Ensure that regional health establishments have an order function, but at the same time they own health establishments in their region, and they are responsible for the performance function of the State. In the event of a deadline breach, the order function of the HELFO Patient Service, which is an external establishment in the Health Directorate, is outside the regional health establishments. The order and delivery roles are then more clearly separated. The reason why HELFO provides the deadline provider with one day to find a new treatment date is that most patients prefer healthcare to be provided in the neighbourhood. According to HELFO, this was successful for approx. half of the 2009 deadline breaks. The look-up in the Evening Post in February showed that only a few of the time-limit patients are seeking HELFO treatment. There may be several reasons why there are no more people who use this option: the patient may not have received sufficient information about his rights in the event of a breach of time or that the patient prefers to wait for treatment at his own hospital. In my view, changes in the organisation of specialist healthcare will not necessarily result in better patient rights being respected. I believe that good internal control and risk management are essential to prevent, prevent and detect deviations, including in the case of a breach of deadlines. At the business meetings in January, I demanded that regional health companies ensure that hospitals undergo referral processing systems and procedures. All regional health companies have reported to me that no findings suggest systematic manipulation of the deadline. A large number of cases have been examined in all regions and it appears that the deadline has changed in 0.07 to 1.5 pst. of the cases. The review in Health South-East is based on information from all health establishments, except Hospital Asker and Bærum. As an example, the Hospital Inland HF has been subject to 65,535 cases from the last year, and they found 46 changes to the deadline. Health Nord has mapped approx. 500000 references, and only in Health Bergen have they been subjected to approx. Twenty thousand. Regional health enterprises have reported several explanations as to why the date had changed. The most important explanations were: misspelling in connection with the date installation, that some systems do not allow misspellings to be corrected, that the change had been made as desired from and in consultation with the patient, that the diagnosis had changed, that could be immediate help insertions or the move of the date in time. Hospitals are reported to have systems and procedures for handling referrals. Regional health businesses believe that information on patients’ rights needs to be improved. Health Mid-Norway reports that they have challenges with which patients should be granted rights status. I have assured myself that regional health companies are following up on this. Health oversight has carried out more supervision of compliance with the Patient Rights Act. These inspections and decisions in complaints have shown that the law is not always complied with. In addition, a study has shown that only a few patients use their rights in the event of a deadline breach. I can't give a sure answer as to why it's like this, or how big the problem really is. Claims have been made that patients are being inadequately informed about their rights, that the legislation is too complicated and too little known, or that patients choose to wait for treatment at their local hospital. The Health Directorate has prepared priority counselling and letter templates for summoning and informing patients' rights. These are now being used in healthcare companies, and I expect this to help ensure that patients' rights are respected. Following the case at the Hospital Asker and Bærum, a number of measures have been taken. Regional health businesses are working on how they can improve their ability to inform patients about their rights. I will continue to assess whether recent measures are effective means of better compliance with patients’ rights. If it turns out that these measures are not sufficient to improve the situation, I will consider additional measures, including: the evaluation and amendment of the legislation. The compliance of healthcare with patient rights is a prerequisite for prioritising those who need it the most, and also for providing an equivalent treatment offer.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e2736	The aim of the Patient Rights Act is to help ensure equal access to good quality healthcare for the general public by giving patients rights to healthcare. The provisions of the law shall contribute to the promotion of patient-health trust and respect for the lives, integrity and human dignity of the individual patient. Today's interpellation raises an important principleal debate on how well we are in practice able to follow up the purpose of the law, and a more specific question, whether there are practices that indicate failure. The relevant cases are dealt with by the supervisory authority and investigated by the police. This citizen for the necessary investigative competence to be placed on the agenda and for independent factual review and conclusion. As long as the cases are investigated by the police, I will not comment on these, but we should remember that the usual legal principles also apply in matters of this nature. Hospitals are large organisations, and only a few people have roles and duties to address the issues highlighted in the cases mentioned. The working day in a hospital is physically and mentally demanding. I want to pay tribute to all the employees who keep their moods up, who give inspiration, for the benefit of their patients and their colleagues, even though demanding and serious issues lie unsolved above the organisation. The background to the right to necessary health care is that conditions and diseases have different natural processes. The old house doctors used observation as an active tool, because some diseases are best passed on by themselves. On the other hand, it is important to get to grips with modern treatment of many diseases while still at an early stage, so that chances of recovery are good. That's why we've created a system based on a medical-professional assessment alone, to determine the urgency. If the responsible doctor sets the deadline to e.g. two weeks, this is legally binding on the hospital. If the time limit is exceeded, the patient will be ordered on request from HELFO Patient Service elsewhere, and this will have to be paid by the hospital that has missed the deadline. The current law reflects the professional ethics which, in my experience, is based in the sector. There are time-limits, but only a few time-limits apply to HELFO for treatment elsewhere, public or private, paid by the hospital behind the breach. We don't have enough knowledge of why this happens. We assume that some patients still prefer treatment as close to home as possible. Adequate information on the consistency and alternatives of deadlines breaks is a necessary part of the quality of the treatment. The deadline was set on the basis of the best medical professional assessments, based on available information. Therefore, it may be to advise that the patient chooses to ignore the deadline. It is also important that the information ensures that the treatment in another hospital is professionally equivalent to the local supply. It is important that the Health Directorate has now set up priority counsellors and letter templates for calling and informing patients' rights. All stages of this chain must be quality assurance. A banal point is that a large group of patients have reduced vision, e.g. letter and information material font sizes are important. We shall await the outcome of the police investigation into the matter in question. But in a complex service, it is important to have good internal control procedures, have an open culture for reporting errors and having the ability to learn from errors, so that they do not happen again.

It is a system that shows how hospitals can detect diagnosis, so that they get the highest possible ignition. One is best paid if the most expensive diagnosis is considered to be the main diagnosis. In my view, such systems have been used in several health services, showing that the level of input-driven funding has been reduced to the level of the department. In the Soria Moria Declaration, the government has announced a review of the health care funding system. The Interaction Reform has already proposed reducing input-driven funding from 40 pst. to 30 pst., something that the Center Party thinks is a change in the right direction. After listening to the debate, I'm becoming more and more disgusted about how agile and complex a system we've created so that patients can easily get the help they need, when they need it. We make ordering and performing functions, and then we criticize these. We create patient rights and deadlines, and then we criticize that they are not followed up. The centre party takes seriously the suspicion that economic considerations may be based on a medical/professional principle – at least on a case-by-case basis. But today's debate I think is actually superficial, it is political, and it is a little constructive.

Municipalities may award care allowances, but are not obliged by law to do so. So it's a judgement for the municipalities. This concerns both the allocation issue and the level of remuneration. From research teams, it is estimated to be close to the size of the municipal care service. The government will further develop the interaction between the public sector and voluntary actors and non-profit institutions serving households. They are important contributors to welfare production. Voluntary efforts by relatives shall remain essentially complementary to the municipal care service. This is what we normally call 'unpaid family care'. In the 2015 care plan, we have an important strategy to ensure a good partnership and to facilitate such voluntary care. It is about providing relief, information, guidance and good interaction on customised solutions. In this context, it is important that the service is organised against – and not out of – family and community.

I don't think anyone here wants a society where everything is to be weighed in money, and everything that's to be done for others, especially those we're close, is to be paid. But we have the change in society that we have, and we know that many are lonely, many are alone, many are without close family in the community, and many have busy family members in full-time work who cannot or do not want to take responsibility for their – or do not heel to do so. This is because the municipality has to take responsibility for the offer to be made, and many get the help. The day is long and demanding, even for those in need, and pay for care can be an opportunity to meet the needs beyond what domestic and other services provide. The provision of related knowledge and encouragement, the availability of good discharge places and the care of relatives, as I said earlier, is important. For most people, living at home is something of the most important and the very best. As politicians, we should facilitate this. The Councils of State referred to St.meld. No 25 for 2005–2006, Mestring, opportunities and meaning, which states that it is necessary to further develop the arrangements we have, so that we can combine working on caring for nursing children, adults and the elderly. I believe that this is important in order to get everyday life together for the relatives. It's important for people to have a job to go to, but also to be able to combine it with caring for their loved ones. We're all going to experience that in periods of our lives we have to take extra care of our loved ones to make everyday life go on. That is the responsibility we have when we have a family, but it should not be expected, or we should have a bad conscience if we do not. But we have to make sure that the systems that are available work and that they catch up and help. Therefore, care pay is one of the things that can be a good tool. That's why I'm happy with what the Council of State says, that it needs to take a look now to see what can be done better. It's too bad routines that the Health Authority has notified. We're looking forward to the sample coming up with its recommendations, and maybe also the definition of what is burdensome care will lie there. Then people can do their everyday life more easily, and we can get a better society for those who need it.

The Progress Party has repeatedly called for improvements in the care pay system. We have made proposals to remove the curtailment of pensioners. We have wanted to extend the scheme to several groups, including: that dependants can provide care for their own parents or partners under illness, and we have proposed that care pay be converted into a government scheme with a normal amount, just to help bring Norway together into a health realm and ensure an equal and predictable system throughout the country. When I hear politicians and bureaucrats say that care pay is a public expenditure, I wish I had the opportunity to send them all on a small economic course with one theme, namely alternative cost. Norwegian society is facing enormous challenges in the future. Everyone is concerned about whether we get enough hands and skilled professionals to do the public health and care tasks. Let's start by improving the care pay system in line with the proposals of the Progress Party. We will then have reduced red tape, we will have many public hands released, and we will have a much better interaction with the private and absolutely necessary care. It is now time for the Government to show adequate care for care providers and to provide a good and fair compensation system for the work done.

ParlaMint-NO_2010-03-02.ana.ud1947e3058	For many families with disabled children, care allowance is a necessary income for the care of the children. No one can replace the care that engaged parents give their children, and these parents make a huge effort. Recently, Nav has tightened up the practice of providing care to many of these parents. This has dramatic and heartbreaking consequences for families. Some of the families affected have received support from health services for intensive training of children with disabilities. These treatments require extensive training, which requires very close parental follow-up. In 2003, the Riksstrøgdeverket gave an opinion that care can be provided under Section 9-10 of the Law on Social Security for Parents with Children treated with the Doman method. It appears from the opinion that care can be provided both during stay at the Doman Institute and after discharge if children need continuous follow-up from one of the parents. Several parents have also received care allowance according to § 9-11. However, Nav is changing the practice of providing care to parents with children who are being treated with the Doman method. A letter from Nav to a parent states that a reassessment of the so-called Doman cases has been considered, suggesting that care is not granted unless the child has an unstable or progressive disease. For the families concerned, the withdrawal of care allowance will make it very difficult, and for several impossible, to continue the training they have set up in cooperation with health care. Parents therefore fear that the positive development they see in their children will stagnate or reverse, thus losing their ability to develop their abilities and potential. At the same time, families lose a source of income that allows them to care for their children daily. This hits the children and the families hard. These parents are referred to as applying for a care allowance, but it is highly uncertain whether they will be granted it and the aid may be limited. It seems unreasonable to be able to make internal changes in Nav practices that have such a serious impact on users’ rights and living conditions. I have raised this matter with former Minister of Labour Rigmor Aasrud, who informed that Nav was entitled to make these changes in practice for the grant of care allowance. However, it is fully possible for the Government to introduce rules ensuring that families who have already received these benefits are not affected by the change in practice. Families with disabled children and others should receive a comprehensive assistance from public welfare services. It is unacceptable for public authorities to introduce rules that impede an already demanding situation in life. The government is talking a lot about interactions in healthcare. It would have been nice if the Minister of Health and Health could practise a little interaction in the Government and talk to his fellow State Councillor Hanne Bjurstrøm about this matter. It should be possible for the Government to instruct Nav to continue the care of families who have already received this, so that they are not affected by the sudden change in practice. This will cost society little, but it will be enormously valuable for families with disabled children. If the government has intended to insist that these families seek care pay, I will challenge the Council of State to answer what she will do to ensure that these families are actually granted such a benefit. Otherwise, established training arrangements for children will fall away, thus weakening their chances of positive development. In addition, children will experience that their parents can no longer take care of them because they no longer have income to cover their expenses. The alternative may be housing, which may be a poorer and more expensive alternative. I challenge the Council of State to take a closer look at the situation of these families and make sure that they do not experience that society is making a difficult situation even more difficult. So finally, the Council of State said in its statement that she would set up an external sample to review care pay. Then she has to bring the users, the parents who know where the shoe is pressing. Those who experience everyday life with e.g. a highly disabled child, should and must be included in such a selection.
